**Corrections made during implementation** (found by re-reading this
section sceptically, per the standing instruction after eleven prior
tasks each shipped a plan defect; none of these were caught by a
separate review pass, only by treating the plan's own code as a draft
rather than a spec):

1. **`requireProjectAccess`'s own snippet above contradicted this
   section's prose.** The prose says build it on `Caller.ScopedProject()`
   and `Caller.IsToken()`, "rather than `Caller`'s raw `TokenID`/
   `ProjectID` fields" — but the code block above read `caller.ProjectID`
   directly. Implemented to match the prose (and Task 10's own doc
   comment on `ScopedProject`, which says the same thing): `if scoped, ok
   := caller.ScopedProject(); ok { ... }`.
2. **`handleListGames` and `handleCreateGame` were reachable by a token
   caller in the plan's own code**, and `handleListGames` in particular
   would have been a scope leak, not merely a wrong-caller nuisance:
   `Projects.ListForUser(ctx, caller.UserID)` returns every game the
   token's *creator* belongs to, not just the one game the token itself
   is bound to — a token minted for one game could enumerate the names of
   every other game its creator happens to also be a member of, exactly
   the cross-game inference `RoleOf` and `BySlugForUser` (Task 8) go out
   of their way to prevent. `requireHumanCaller` (renamed from a
   membership-only helper — see item 3) now gates both.
3. **`requireHumanCaller` widened from "no membership management" to "no
   game, token or membership management".** The plan defined it only for
   `handleListMembers`/`handleChangeRole`/`handleRemoveMember`, with a
   doc comment reasoning that a bearer token authenticates an agent
   scoped to content, not a person entitled to decide who else manages
   the game. That reasoning applies identically to creating or listing
   games (item 2) and to minting, listing or revoking tokens (item 4) —
   an agent is not entitled to manage other credentials in its own
   project either, itself included. One helper, one doc comment, applied
   at the top of every handler in `api_projects.go` and `api_tokens.go`
   except none — there is no game- or token-management route left that a
   token caller may reach.
4. **Token creation had no role gate at all in the plan** — any project
   member, viewer included, could mint a token. A token grants an agent
   whatever standing its project binding carries, for as long as the
   token lives, independent of the person who minted it; a viewer is
   someone an owner deliberately gave read-only access to, so letting
   them mint a full-access credential is a privilege escalation dressed
   as a convenience. `handleCreateToken` now requires editor or owner
   (`requireTokenMinter`, `internal/web/api_tokens.go`), backed by a new
   `roles.AtLeast(role, min)` (`internal/roles/roles.go`) — the package
   had no ordering between its three roles before this, only `Valid`.
   Revocation was considered for the same gate and deliberately left
   open to any member instead: revoking only ever *removes* access, so
   anyone who can already see a leaked token in the listing (any member —
   see item 5) can kill it; gating that too would make a viewer who
   spots a compromised token unable to act on what they can see.
5. **Listing tokens and listing members stay open to every project role,
   viewer included — confirmed, not changed.** Neither leaks a live
   secret (`ListAPITokens` never returns a token's value or hash, only
   label/hint/timestamps; `ListMembers` never returns email, per Task 8's
   own corrections) and both are read access to information already
   scoped to a game the caller is a member of, matching the precedent
   `ListMembers`'s own doc comment already set.
6. **`handleCreateGame` mapped only `ErrSlugTaken`, sending every other
   `projects.Create` validation failure — an invalid slug shape, an
   invalid name — to a generic 500.** Both are ordinary 422s
   (`projects.ErrSlugInvalid`, `projects.ErrNameInvalid`), the same class
   of defect Task 11's own corrections fixed for registration
   (`ErrEmailInvalid` etc. mapped to 422 instead of falling through to
   the default branch). Fixed by the same pattern: a `switch` over
   `errors.Is` before the generic fallback.
   `TestCreateGameRejectsInvalidSlugAsBadRequest` pins it.
7. **`handleChangeRole` did not map `projects.ErrUserNotFound`**, so
   granting a role to a nonexistent user id fell through to a generic
   500 instead of the 404 the error exists to enable (see `ErrUserNotFound`'s
   own doc comment in `projects.go`, which names this exact handler as
   the reason it was mapped by constraint name rather than left a raw
   `23503`). Added alongside the other `SetRole` error branches.
8. **`handleRoot` redirected an anonymous caller to `/login` but did
   nothing explicit about a token caller reaching `/`** — a bearer
   token has no session to speak of and nothing to redirect to
   meaningfully, so it is folded into the same anonymous branch
   (`!ok || caller.IsToken()`) rather than left to fall through to
   `ListForUser(ctx, caller.UserID)`, which would have hit the same
   cross-game leak as item 2 for the human behind the token.
   `handleRoot`'s doc comment also now says explicitly what happens for a
   member of zero games: the picker/shell branch, not a special case,
   since `len(games) == 1` is the only condition that shortcuts — zero and
   two-or-more both fall through to the same `serveAsset` call, and the
   empty-state prompt for a brand-new user is the SPA's job (a later
   task), not this handler's. `TestRootShowsPickerWithNoGames` and
   `TestRootRedirectsAnonymousToLogin` pin both.
9. **A "last visited game" redirect, described in the task brief handed
   to the implementer, does not appear anywhere in this plan section and
   was not built.** Checked deliberately, not assumed: neither this
   section's prose nor its tests (`TestRootShowsPickerWithTwoGames`
   asserts a bare 200 for two games, with no cookie set and no assertion
   on which game the picker would default to) describe where such a
   preference would be stored, read, or take precedence over the picker.
   Storing it server-side would need a new column and a write on every
   navigation for a feature this section never specified the shape of;
   storing it client-side (`localStorage`, read by the SPA before it
   ever calls `GET /`) needs no backend change at all and is the more
   likely home for it once the SPA exists (Task 14 onward). Left
   unbuilt rather than guessed at, the same choice Correction 12 of Task
   8 made for `/g/{slug}` resolution: named here so it is a known gap
   with an owner, not a silent one a later task rediscovers. **Assigned:
   Task 15 now owns it** — `localStorage`, keyed `maestro:lastGame`,
   read only after `GET /api/games` answers and redirecting only when
   the remembered slug appears in that live response; see Task 15's own
   section for the full rule and why a server column was rejected.
10. **`DELETE /api/games/{game}` — confirmed still out of scope, per this
    section's own prose above ("This endpoint is not implemented in this
    task; it is named here so the gap has an owner").** Not built. The
    gap it leaves (a designer who creates a game alone still cannot
    delete it, only leave-if-not-sole-owner via `handleRemoveMember`)
    is unchanged from Task 8's Round 2 Correction 9 and remains a named
    item for a future task, not this one. **Assigned: Task 17 now owns
    it** — owner-only, mapping `ErrLastOwner` the same 409 way this
    task's own membership handlers already do; see that task's section
    for why a plain single-statement `DELETE FROM projects` is enough
    (the cascade already exists in migration 0001, and migration 0002's
    trigger has an escape hatch built for exactly this statement).
11. **Test file grew from the plan's seven tests to twenty-one**, adding:
    the token-caller and role-gate coverage for items 2-4 (`TestListGamesRejectsTokenCaller`,
    `TestCreateGameRejectsTokenCaller`, `TestViewerCannotCreateToken`,
    `TestViewerCanRevokeToken`, `TestTokenEndpointsRejectTokenCaller`);
    the validation-mapping coverage for item 6
    (`TestCreateGameRejectsInvalidSlugAsBadRequest`); the root-navigation
    edge cases for item 8 (`TestRootShowsPickerWithNoGames`,
    `TestRootRedirectsAnonymousToLogin`); `TestListMembersNeverLeaksEmail`,
    reading the response body directly rather than trusting `Member`'s
    shape by inspection; `TestChangeRoleOnSoleOwnerReportsLastOwner`,
    isolating the 409 mapping from `TestOnlyOwnerCanChangeRole`'s
    already-crowded scenario; and `TestNonOwnerCannotRemoveAnotherMember`,
    which the plan's own `TestMemberCanRemoveSelfButNotSoleOwner` never
    exercised (it only ever removes the caller's own membership).

**Round 2 corrections** (a quality review of the committed Round 1 code,
run against commit `2f91bc6`, after the corrections above had already
landed and Task 17 had been named in this document as game deletion's
owner):

1. **`decodeAuthBody` (Task 11) was never used by the three JSON-body
   handlers this task added.** `handleCreateGame`, `handleChangeRole` and
   `handleCreateToken` all called `json.NewDecoder(r.Body).Decode`
   directly — no content-type check, no size bound — so any authenticated
   project member, viewer included, could post an unbounded body to
   `POST /api/games` and have it decoded into memory before
   `projects.Create`'s own validation ever saw it, exactly the failure
   mode `decodeAuthBody`'s own doc comment exists to prevent. Renamed to
   `decodeJSONBody` (it was never auth-specific in the first place) and
   used by all three; `api_auth.go`'s two original call sites carried
   over unchanged.
2. **`requireProjectAccess` became a wrapper, not a helper each handler
   called.** The previous shape — a `(uuid.UUID, bool)`-returning helper
   every project-scoped handler had to remember to invoke — compiled
   fine for a handler that forgot to call it, silently serving whatever
   the `{game}` path segment named with no scope check at all; and every
   role-gated handler (`handleChangeRole`, `handleRemoveMember`,
   `handleCreateToken`) called `RoleOf` a *second* time after the one the
   helper already did, a wasted round trip and a real
   time-of-check-to-time-of-use window in which a demotion landing
   between the two lookups was still admitted by the first. Replaced by
   `requireProject`, which wraps a handler and hands it a `ProjectScope`
   (`internal/web/api_projects.go`) carrying the resolved project id, the
   caller's role, and whether it arrived by token — a parameter with no
   constructor reachable from outside this file, so a project-scoped
   handler cannot be written without going through the check that
   produces one. Registered as `requireCaller(s.requireProject(handler))`
   for all six project-scoped routes this task owns (`handleListMembers`,
   `handleChangeRole`, `handleRemoveMember`, `handleCreateToken`,
   `handleListTokens`, `handleRevokeToken`); Task 17's `handleDeleteGame`
   is written against this shape from the start (see that task's own
   section, amended below).
3. **The token role model, decided:** a token is editor-equivalent and
   carries no role of its own; it never exceeds its minter's *role at
   the time of their last role change*, not their live, current one.
   `requireProject` sets a token caller's `ProjectScope.Role` to
   `roles.Editor` unconditionally, never from a membership lookup —
   looking it up live would mean an already-issued token's access
   silently changes whenever its minter's own role does, backwards from
   "a token outlives the person who made it" (this task's own original
   design note). What keeps that safe without a per-request lookup:
   `projects.SetRole` now revokes every token a member minted in that
   project the moment their new role drops below editor (mirroring what
   `RemoveMember` already does for a lost membership entirely), via the
   same `RevokeAPITokensForMember` query — widened from `:exec` to
   `:many` (`RETURNING label`, see item 10) and now called from both
   methods. Promoting, or moving between editor and owner, never
   triggers it. `TestSetRoleDemotionBelowEditorRevokesTheDemotedMembersTokens`
   and a mixed-race test (below) pin this.
4. **`handleListTokens` passed `identity.APITokenSummary` straight to
   `writeJSON`.** `APITokenSummary`'s own doc comment says nothing should
   be one careless `writeJSON` call away from serving a field like the
   token hash; this was that call, saved only by the hash already being
   absent from the struct — every other field, `UserIsAdmin` and
   PascalCase JSON keys included, still went out verbatim, the one
   listing in this package not built from a hand-written snake_case
   shape like every sibling handler. Replaced with `apiTokenResponse`, a
   typed struct naming exactly `id`, `label`, `token_hint`, `minted_by`,
   `created_at`, `last_used_at` and `revoked_at`; `UserIsAdmin` is
   dropped, not renamed — a token listing has no business reporting
   whether its minter is an instance admin.
5. **An over-long token label returned 500.** `identity.ErrTokenRequestInvalid`
   was unmapped in `handleCreateToken`, unlike `handleCreateGame`'s own
   handling of the analogous `projects.ErrNameInvalid`. Mapped to 422
   (`errCodeLabelInvalid`).
6. **None of the eight new 500 paths logged anything server-side**,
   unlike every 500 path in `api_auth.go` (Task 11's own corrections
   added exactly this there). Every `errCodeInternal` branch in
   `api_projects.go` and `api_tokens.go` now calls `slog.ErrorContext`
   with the underlying error and the project id (and, where relevant,
   the target user or token id) before writing the generic response.
   **This claim was itself wrong as originally written** — see Round 3,
   Correction 3 below: it covered only the two files this task touched
   and missed `authenticate`'s two pre-existing silent 500 paths in
   `auth.go`, which predate this task and so were out of its scope on
   the first pass. Fixed in Round 3; the claim now correctly covers the
   whole package, not just this task's own two files.
7. **`handleRoot` carried neither `Cache-Control: no-store` nor `Vary`,**
   despite being the most identity-dependent response in the product — a
   redirect to one game for one caller, to `/login` for another, to the
   picker shell for a third. It bypasses `requireCaller` on purpose (an
   anonymous caller has to become a redirect to `/login`, not a 401), so
   it never got the headers `requireCaller` sets for every handler it
   wraps. `setNoStoreHeaders` (`auth.go`) now holds that header pair as
   its own function, called by both `requireCaller` and `handleRoot`
   directly, so the two paths cannot drift. `handleRoot`'s one failure
   path (the `ListForUser` error) also switched from `writeError` (a JSON
   body) to `http.Error` (plain text): this route only ever serves an
   HTML navigation, and a JSON error body was the one response shape on
   it that did not match.
8. **(Critical, in the sense that it made every role check in this file
   only as strong as its weakest threshold.)** `roles.AtLeast` looked up
   `role` in its rank table and correctly failed closed for an unknown
   value, but looked up `min` with a bare map index whose missing-key
   zero value (0, `Viewer`'s rank) let an unrecognised **threshold**
   silently rank as the *lowest* one instead of failing at all —
   `AtLeast(Viewer, "auditor")` returned `true`, making a typo in the
   threshold argument the most permissive possible check instead of the
   most restrictive. `AtLeast` now looks up both `role` and `min` and
   returns `false` unless both are recognised.
9. **`internal/roles` had no test file, and its own package doc comment
   cited a test — `roles_test.go`'s `TestAllRolesAcceptedByDatabase` —
   that had never lived there.** That test exists (in
   `internal/projects/projects_test.go`, since proving database agreement
   needs a live pool and a project to insert memberships against, which
   this package deliberately depends on nothing to get); the doc comment
   now says so correctly. `internal/roles/roles_test.go` is new, covering
   the pure-Go half: `TestValid` and a `TestAtLeast` table over every
   role-and-threshold pair, including both positions holding an
   unrecognised value — the table that would have caught item 8
   immediately had it existed first.
10. **`RemoveMember` answered 204 after silently revoking a removed
    member's tokens**, leaving the owner who removed them with no way to
    know which of their agents had just stopped working — a revoked
    token id means nothing on its own. `RemoveMember`'s signature is now
    `(ctx, userID, projectID) ([]string, error)`, returning the labels of
    every token it revoked (never nil — an empty slice, not JSON `null`,
    for a removal that revoked nothing); `handleRemoveMember` answers
    `200 {"revoked_tokens": [...]}` instead of a bare 204.
    `RevokeAPITokensForMember`'s query gained `RETURNING label` for
    exactly this (see item 3 for its second caller).
11. **Two tests asserted a status code and nothing else, which is not
    the same as proving the behaviour happened.** The revocation test
    (`TestViewerCanRevokeToken`) asserted 204 only — it would have passed
    against a handler that was a bare `WriteHeader(204)` with the actual
    revoke call deleted. It now mints a token over HTTP, revokes it over
    HTTP, then uses that same bearer value against `/api/me` and requires
    401. The token-creation test
    (`TestTokenIsReturnedOnceOnCreation`) asserted the clear value was
    absent from the listing but never that the listing contained the
    created row at all — it would have passed against an empty list. It
    now also decodes the listing and asserts the created id is present.
    A new `TestRevokingAnUnknownOrForeignTokenIsANoop` pins the
    deliberate 204 (not 404) for a token id that does not exist or
    belongs to a different project — see item 12. A new
    `TestConcurrentRemovalAndDemotionOfDifferentOwnersLeavesExactlyOneOwner`
    (`internal/projects/projects_test.go`) drives the mixed race neither
    the existing `TestConcurrentRemovalLeavesExactlyOneOwner` (both
    goroutines call `RemoveMember`) nor any `SetRole` test alone
    exercised: one goroutine removing owner A while a second demotes
    owner B, concurrently — the same "exactly one wins, zero owners never
    happens" invariant, now proven across the mix of both methods, not
    just within either alone.
12. **Minors, all applied:** the bare `"owner"` string comparisons in
    `handleChangeRole` and `handleRemoveMember` are now
    `roles.AtLeast(roles.Role(scope.Role), roles.Owner)`; the
    `"role must be one of owner, editor, viewer"` message is now built
    from `roles.All()` (`roleList()`) so it cannot drift from the
    vocabulary `roles.Valid` actually checks; the error-code constants in
    `auth.go` grew from two to twelve, and every inline string literal
    this task's two handler files used to spell (`"forbidden"`,
    `"not_found"`, `"bad_request"`, `"scope_violation"`, `"slug_taken"`,
    `"slug_invalid"`, `"name_invalid"`, `"invalid_role"`, `"last_owner"`,
    plus the new `"label_invalid"`) now reads one of them instead;
    `handleChangeRole` now maps `projects.ErrProjectNotFound` (unreachable
    today, reachable the moment Task 17 ships); token creation now
    returns `token_hint` in its response, not just in the listing, so an
    operator has the fingerprint to match a leaked value against right
    when they mint it; `TestRevokingAnUnknownOrForeignTokenIsANoop`
    (item 11) doubles as the comment-plus-test pin the review asked for
    on `handleRevokeToken`'s deliberate no-op shape; `requireHumanCaller`
    is a package-level function, not a method — it used neither a
    receiver nor the request, only the already-resolved `Caller`.
13. **Ids, not slugs, in the API — written down as a decision, not left
    an accident.** Every route this task and Task 17 add takes a project
    id (a UUID) in its path, never a slug; `POST /api/games`'s response
    and `GET /api/games`'s listing are where a client learns the id for
    a slug it already knows. This was true of the code from the first
    commit but was never stated as a deliberate choice until this review
    asked the question directly. The reasoning: an id is stable across a
    rename (Task 8's `Project.Slug` is mutable in principle, even though
    nothing yet exposes a rename endpoint) and the REST API has no
    reason to care what a game is currently called; `/g/{slug}` stays
    the human-facing URL shape (Task 8's Round 2 Correction 12), and the
    SPA is what maps between the two, once, from `GET /api/games`'s own
    response — not a resolution this task's JSON API needs to perform on
    every request.

**Round 3 corrections** (a re-review of the committed Round 2 code,
which verified the round by attacking it — measuring exactly one
`RoleOf` call per request with a query tracer, confirming 413/415 on all
three JSON-body endpoints, demoting a token's minter over HTTP and
calling the token again, and running both strengthened tests against a
deliberately broken subject to confirm they actually go red):

1. **`ProjectScope`'s doc comment claimed something false, and the
   reviewer proved it false.** It said a `ProjectScope` "cannot be
   fabricated... there is no other way to construct it from outside this
   file". The reviewer wrote a handler in this same package —
   `package web`, same as every handler this task added — that parsed
   `r.PathValue("game")` itself, called `s.opts.Projects.ListMembers`
   directly with no `ProjectScope` anywhere in its signature, registered
   it on the mux, and served a game's membership list to a caller with
   no membership in it. It compiled and it worked, because Go's
   visibility rules stop at the package boundary: nothing about an
   unexported struct stops a same-package function from ignoring it
   entirely, no matter how the struct's fields are named or gated. This
   is the fourth overclaiming comment this plan has had corrected by a
   review across its tasks so far — worth naming because each one told a
   future reader a boundary was enforced when it was only conventional.
   `ProjectScope`'s comment now says plainly what it does and does not
   guarantee: it resolves scope once (closing the time-of-check window
   Round 2 fixed), and it makes the guarded path the only *ergonomic*
   one, not the only *possible* one.
   Actual enforcement moved to where Go can actually check something:
   `registerProjectRoute` (`server.go`) is now the only call in `NewServer`
   allowed to wire up a route whose pattern contains `{game}` — it
   records every pattern it registers in `s.projectScopedPatterns` — and
   every route registration in `NewServer` (project-scoped or not) goes
   through `route`/`routeFunc`, which records the pattern in
   `s.registeredPatterns` regardless of which helper wired it up.
   `TestEveryGameScopedRouteGoesThroughRequireProject`
   (`server_test.go`) walks `registeredPatterns` and fails if any pattern
   containing `{game}` is missing from `projectScopedPatterns` — turning
   "someone might register a `{game}` route by hand" into a failing test
   instead of a comment nobody re-reads. This was checked to actually
   catch the failure mode, not just checked to compile: a temporary
   `s.route("GET /api/games/{game}/sneaky", ...)` inserted directly
   (bypassing `registerProjectRoute`) made the test fail with exactly
   the expected message, then was removed.
2. **Demotion revoked tokens and told nobody — the same gap Round 2
   closed for removal, still open for the other path that kills a
   member's agents.** `projects.SetRole` revoked a demoted member's
   tokens correctly (Round 2, Correction 3) but its signature stayed
   `(ctx, userID, projectID, role) error`, so `handleChangeRole` answered
   a bare `200` with an empty body after silently killing the demoted
   member's agents — the reviewer minted a token, demoted its minter
   over HTTP, got nothing back, and only found out the token was dead by
   calling it again. `SetRole`'s signature is now
   `(ctx, userID, projectID, role) ([]string, error)`, returning the
   labels of every token it revoked (never nil, the same convention
   `RemoveMember` already follows); `handleChangeRole` answers
   `200 {"revoked_tokens": [...]}` instead of a bare `200`, mirroring
   `handleRemoveMember`'s own response shape exactly, per this task's own
   original note that demotion mirrors removal.
   `TestChangeRoleDemotionReportsRevokedTokenLabels`
   (`api_projects_test.go`) pins it at the HTTP layer, alongside
   `internal/projects/projects_test.go`'s existing
   `TestSetRoleDemotionBelowEditorRevokesTheDemotedMembersTokens`, now
   asserting on the returned labels directly rather than only on the
   token's liveness afterward.
3. **`authenticate`'s two 500 paths (`auth.go`, predating this task) were
   silent**, contradicting Round 2 Correction 6's claim that every 500
   path in this package logs — that claim was accurate only for the two
   files this task touched, and was written as though it covered the
   whole package. `resolveBearerCaller`'s and `resolveSessionCaller`'s
   database-failure branches inside `authenticate` now call
   `slog.ErrorContext` before writing the generic 500, matching every
   other 500 path in `internal/web`; Round 2's Correction 6 above is
   amended in place to say so, so a future reader does not have to find
   this correction to learn the earlier claim was too narrow.

### Task 13: MCP surface with whoami and games, and scope enforcement

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/web/mcp.go`
- Modify: `internal/web/server.go`
- Test: `internal/web/mcp_test.go`

- [ ] **Step 1: Add the dependency**

```bash
go get github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk@latest
```

- [ ] **Step 2: Write the failing test**

`internal/web/mcp_test.go`:

```go
package web_test

import (
	"context"
	"testing"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/web"
)

func TestMCPWhoamiReportsTheTokenProject(t *testing.T) {
	_, ids, projSvc := newTestServer(t)
	ctx := context.Background()

	user, _ := ids.CreateUser(ctx, identity.CreateUserRequest{Email: "designer@studio.com", DisplayName: "Designer", Password: "password12345"})
	project, _ := projSvc.Create(ctx, "azeroth", "Azeroth", user.ID)
	token, _, _ := ids.CreateAPIToken(ctx, identity.CreateAPITokenRequest{ProjectID: project.ID, UserID: user.ID, Label: "agent"})

	caller, err := web.CallerForToken(ctx, ids, token)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("CallerForToken: %v", err)
	}

	out, err := web.MCPWhoami(ctx, web.MCPDeps{Identity: ids, Projects: projSvc}, caller)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("MCPWhoami: %v", err)
	}
	if out.UserID != user.ID {
		t.Fatal("whoami reported the wrong user")
	}
	if out.ProjectID == nil || *out.ProjectID != project.ID {
		t.Fatal("whoami did not report the token's project")
	}
	if out.ProjectSlug != "azeroth" {
		t.Fatalf("ProjectSlug = %q, want azeroth", out.ProjectSlug)
	}
}

func TestMCPGamesGetRefusesAnotherProject(t *testing.T) {
	_, ids, projSvc := newTestServer(t)
	ctx := context.Background()

	user, _ := ids.CreateUser(ctx, identity.CreateUserRequest{Email: "designer@studio.com", DisplayName: "Designer", Password: "password12345"})
	mine, _ := projSvc.Create(ctx, "azeroth", "Azeroth", user.ID)
	theirs, _ := projSvc.Create(ctx, "le-mans", "Le Mans", user.ID)
	token, _, _ := ids.CreateAPIToken(ctx, identity.CreateAPITokenRequest{ProjectID: mine.ID, UserID: user.ID, Label: "agent"})

	caller, err := web.CallerForToken(ctx, ids, token)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("CallerForToken: %v", err)
	}

	if _, err := web.MCPGamesGet(ctx, web.MCPDeps{Identity: ids, Projects: projSvc}, caller, theirs.ID); err == nil {
		t.Fatal("a token must not read another game, even one its user owns")
	}
}

func TestMCPGamesGetRefusesAnotherProjectForAdmins(t *testing.T) {
	// This test needs a genuine admin. CreateUser can never mint one (Task
	// 5, Correction 11), so it goes through BootstrapFirstAdmin exactly as
	// production does, which means it needs its own config — testConfig()
	// used by newTestServer deliberately leaves FirstAdminEmail/Password
	// unset so unrelated tests don't get a surprise admin row.
	pool := testutil.NewPool(t)
	cfg := testConfig()
	cfg.FirstAdminEmail = "boss@studio.com"
	cfg.FirstAdminPassword = "password12345"
	ids := identity.New(pool, cfg)
	projSvc := projects.New(pool)
	ctx := context.Background()

	if err := ids.BootstrapFirstAdmin(ctx); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("BootstrapFirstAdmin: %v", err)
	}
	admin, err := ids.Authenticate(ctx, cfg.FirstAdminEmail, cfg.FirstAdminPassword)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("Authenticate admin: %v", err)
	}
	mine, _ := projSvc.Create(ctx, "azeroth", "Azeroth", admin.ID)
	theirs, _ := projSvc.Create(ctx, "le-mans", "Le Mans", admin.ID)
	token, _, _ := ids.CreateAPIToken(ctx, identity.CreateAPITokenRequest{ProjectID: mine.ID, UserID: admin.ID, Label: "admin agent"})

	caller, err := web.CallerForToken(ctx, ids, token)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("CallerForToken: %v", err)
	}
	if _, err := web.MCPGamesGet(ctx, web.MCPDeps{Identity: ids, Projects: projSvc}, caller, theirs.ID); err == nil {
		t.Fatal("admin tokens are not exempt from project scope")
	}
}
```

- [ ] **Step 3: Run the test to verify it fails**

Run: `go test ./internal/web/ -run TestMCP -v`
Expected: FAIL, `undefined: web.CallerForToken`.

- [ ] **Step 4: Write the implementation**

`internal/web/mcp.go`:

```go
package web

import (
	"context"
	"fmt"

	"github.com/google/uuid"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/identity"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/projects"
)

// MCPDeps are the domain services the MCP tools need. Keeping them in one
// struct lets the tool functions be plain, directly testable functions.
type MCPDeps struct {
	Identity *identity.Service
	Projects *projects.Service
}

// WhoamiOutput is the shape returned by whoami.
type WhoamiOutput struct {
	UserID      uuid.UUID  `json:"user_id"`
	DisplayName string     `json:"display_name"`
	IsAdmin     bool       `json:"is_admin"`
	ProjectID   *uuid.UUID `json:"project_id,omitempty"`
	ProjectSlug string     `json:"project_slug,omitempty"`
	ProjectName string     `json:"project_name,omitempty"`
}

// GameOutput is the shape returned by games.get and games.list.
type GameOutput struct {
	ID   uuid.UUID `json:"id"`
	Slug string    `json:"slug"`
	Name string    `json:"name"`
}

// CallerForToken resolves a bearer token to a Caller, for MCP transports and
// for tests that do not go through HTTP.
func CallerForToken(ctx context.Context, ids *identity.Service, token string) (Caller, error) {
	row, err := ids.ResolveAPIToken(ctx, token)
	if err != nil {
		return Caller{}, err
	}
	user, err := ids.UserByID(ctx, row.UserID)
	if err != nil {
		return Caller{}, err
	}
	projectID := row.ProjectID
	return Caller{
		UserID:    row.UserID,
		IsAdmin:   user.IsAdmin,
		TokenID:   &row.ID,
		ProjectID: &projectID,
	}, nil
}

// ErrScopeViolation is returned whenever a caller reaches outside its project.
var ErrScopeViolation = fmt.Errorf("scope_violation: this token is bound to another game")

// requireScope checks a caller may act on a project. A token caller is bound
// to exactly one; being an instance admin does not widen that.
func requireScope(caller Caller, projectID uuid.UUID) error {
	if caller.ProjectID == nil {
		return ErrScopeViolation
	}
	if *caller.ProjectID != projectID {
		return ErrScopeViolation
	}
	return nil
}

// MCPWhoami implements whoami.
func MCPWhoami(ctx context.Context, deps MCPDeps, caller Caller) (WhoamiOutput, error) {
	user, err := deps.Identity.UserByID(ctx, caller.UserID)
	if err != nil {
		return WhoamiOutput{}, err
	}
	out := WhoamiOutput{
		UserID:      user.ID,
		DisplayName: user.DisplayName,
		IsAdmin:     user.IsAdmin,
	}
	if caller.ProjectID != nil {
		project, err := deps.Projects.ByID(ctx, *caller.ProjectID)
		if err != nil {
			return WhoamiOutput{}, err
		}
		out.ProjectID = &project.ID
		out.ProjectSlug = project.Slug
		out.ProjectName = project.Name
	}
	return out, nil
}

// MCPGamesGet implements games.get.
func MCPGamesGet(ctx context.Context, deps MCPDeps, caller Caller, projectID uuid.UUID) (GameOutput, error) {
	if err := requireScope(caller, projectID); err != nil {
		return GameOutput{}, err
	}
	project, err := deps.Projects.ByID(ctx, projectID)
	if err != nil {
		return GameOutput{}, err
	}
	return GameOutput{ID: project.ID, Slug: project.Slug, Name: project.Name}, nil
}

// MCPGamesList implements games.list. A token caller sees exactly the
// one game its token is bound to.
func MCPGamesList(ctx context.Context, deps MCPDeps, caller Caller) ([]GameOutput, error) {
	if caller.ProjectID == nil {
		return nil, ErrScopeViolation
	}
	project, err := deps.Projects.ByID(ctx, *caller.ProjectID)
	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}
	return []GameOutput{{ID: project.ID, Slug: project.Slug, Name: project.Name}}, nil
}
```

// ByID loads one project.
func (s *Service) ByID(ctx context.Context, id uuid.UUID) (dbq.Project, error) {
	project, err := s.q.GetProjectByID(ctx, id)
	if err != nil {
		return dbq.Project{}, fmt.Errorf("lookup project: %w", err)
	}
	return project, nil
}

- [ ] **Step 7: Mount the MCP transport**

Add to `internal/web/mcp.go`:

```go
// mcpHandler serves the MCP endpoint. Every request must carry a bearer
// token; the resolved Caller decides what the tools can see.
func (s *Server) mcpHandler() http.Handler {
	return http.HandlerFunc(func(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {
		caller, ok := CallerFrom(r.Context())
		if !ok || caller.ProjectID == nil {
			writeError(w, http.StatusUnauthorized, "unauthorized", "an api token is required on /mcp")
			return
		}
		s.mcp.ServeHTTP(w, r)
	})
}
```

Wire `s.mcp` in `NewServer` with the official SDK server, registering
`whoami`, `games.list` and `games.get` (unprefixed — see this task's
Correction 9) as thin wrappers
that read the `Caller` from the request context and call `MCPWhoami`,
`MCPGamesList` and `MCPGamesGet`. Register the route:

```go
	s.mux.Handle("/mcp", s.mcpHandler())
```

**Corrections made during implementation** (found by reading this
section sceptically before writing any code, per the standing
instruction after twelve prior tasks each shipped a plan defect):

1. **`projects.ByID` already existed.** Task 12 added it (returning the
   domain `Project` type, mapping a missing row to `ErrProjectNotFound`)
   for its own `requireProject`. Step 5's snippet, which predates that,
   would have redefined it against `dbq.Project` with no not-found
   mapping. Skipped Step 5 entirely and used the existing method and its
   `ErrProjectNotFound`.
2. **`CallerForToken` dropped the second `UserByID` call the plan's
   draft made.** `ResolveAPIToken`'s own row already carries
   `UserIsAdmin` (`APITokenSummary`, `tokens.go`) — `resolveBearerCaller`
   in `auth.go` already relies on this to avoid a second round trip per
   authenticated request. The plan's `CallerForToken` snippet called
   `UserByID` again after `ResolveAPIToken`, which would have
   reintroduced exactly the query Task 9 removed from the hot path, and
   risked disagreeing with `resolveBearerCaller` about what a token
   caller's `IsAdmin` is. Built on `newTokenCaller` directly instead, the
   same constructor `resolveBearerCaller` uses.
3. **`requireScope` was written against `Caller.ScopedProject()`, not
   the raw `ProjectID` field the plan's snippet read directly** — the
   same correction Task 12's plan needed for `requireProjectAccess`, for
   the same reason: `ScopedProject` is the one place "an admin is not
   exempt from a token's binding" is encoded, and reading `ProjectID`
   directly here would re-derive that invariant a second time instead of
   reusing it.
4. **The plan specified "stable machine-readable codes" but never said
   how they reach the wire**, and the obvious approach — returning a
   plain Go `error` from a tool handler — does not produce one: the
   SDK's `ToolHandlerFor` packs a bare error into unstructured text
   content with no code an agent can switch on (its own doc comment says
   so). Added `mcpErrorResult`/`mcpErrorFor` (`mcp.go`), which build a
   `CallToolResult` whose content is `{"error":"<code>","message":"..."}`
   using the same `errCode*` constants and the same codes `writeError`
   already uses for the REST surface (`scope_violation`, `not_found`,
   `internal_error`, `unauthorized`), so an agent parses one error shape
   regardless of which surface answered it.
5. **Every `uuid.UUID`-typed field in `WhoamiOutput`/`GameOutput`, used
   directly as an MCP tool's structured `Out`/`In` type, fails the SDK's
   own schema validation on every call.** `uuid.UUID`'s underlying type
   is `[16]byte`; the SDK's schema inference reflects the Go type
   structurally rather than consulting `MarshalText`/`MarshalJSON`, so it
   infers "array of 16 integers" for a field that actually marshals to a
   string — and `AddTool` validates real output against that inferred
   schema before it ever reaches a client. This was caught by an
   end-to-end test added beyond the plan's three (`TestMCPEndToEndOverHTTP`,
   which drives the real mounted transport with the SDK's own client,
   rather than only calling `MCPWhoami`/`MCPGamesGet` in-process the way
   the plan's own tests do) — `whoami` failed on its very first
   successful call. Fixed by adding wire-only types (`whoamiWire`,
   `gameWire`, `gamesListWire`, and `gamesGetInput.ProjectID` as
   `string`) that every tool registration converts to/from at the
   boundary; `WhoamiOutput`/`GameOutput`/`MCPWhoami`/`MCPGamesGet`/
   `MCPGamesList` themselves are unchanged from the plan and still use
   `uuid.UUID`, matching every test that calls them directly.
6. **The plan never said whether `/mcp` should accept a session
   cookie**, and its own `mcpHandler` snippet checked only
   `caller.ProjectID == nil` rather than `!caller.IsToken()`. Those two
   checks happen to coincide today (only `newTokenCaller` ever sets
   `ProjectID`), but the explicit `IsToken()` check makes the intent —
   "the REST surface is closed to tokens; the MCP surface is closed to
   sessions, the mirror image" — hold by construction rather than by an
   incidental correlation between two fields that a future `Caller` shape
   could break silently. Implemented with `!caller.IsToken()`.
7. **The plan's mounting sketch left the SDK's default (stateful,
   `Mcp-Session-Id`-keyed) session mode in place**, which — like Task
   14's SSE stream — would keep a session's authentication fixed at
   handshake time for as long as the session lived, the exact long-lived-
   connection-versus-per-request-middleware mismatch this task's own
   brief warned about ("what happens to an in-flight MCP call when the
   token is revoked mid-request"). Mounted with
   `StreamableHTTPOptions{Stateless: true}` instead: every tool call is
   its own independently-authenticated POST to `/mcp`, so a revoked token
   blocks the very next call rather than continuing to work for the
   life of a connection. `TestMCPRejectsARevokedTokenOverHTTP` pins this.
8. **Added tests beyond the plan's three**, all in the same spirit as
   the corrections above: `TestRequireScopeRefusesASessionCaller` and
   `TestRequireScopeIgnoresAdmin` (pure, no database) for `requireScope`
   itself; `TestMCPHandlerRefusesAnUnauthenticatedRequest` and
   `TestMCPHandlerRefusesASessionCaller` for the transport's own door,
   independent of whether any tool logic is correct; and
   `TestMCPEndToEndOverHTTP` / `TestMCPRejectsAnUnauthenticatedRequestOverHTTP`
   / `TestMCPRejectsARevokedTokenOverHTTP`, which drive the actual
   mounted `StreamableHTTPHandler` end to end with the SDK's own client
   rather than only the plain Go functions — the class of test that
   caught Correction 5.
9. **Tool names shipped unprefixed** (`whoami`, `games.list`,
   `games.get`), not `maestro.whoami`/`maestro.games.list`/
   `maestro.games.get` as Steps 4 and 7's own snippets (now corrected
   above) still read before this note. An MCP client already namespaces
   every tool by the server it came from — the evidence in front of the
   reviewer who caught this was Nottario's own tools arriving as
   `mcp__nottario__nottario_tasks_create`, the server name duplicated
   inside the tool name — so a `maestro.` prefix on top of that would
   only double the noise, not add information. `Implementation.Name` is
   already `"maestro"`; that is where the namespace lives.

**Round 2, from a quality review that verified every finding against the
SDK directly rather than by reading (probed the actual marshalled JSON
and the actual validation behaviour), not merely against this file:**

10. **(Blocking) Every error result also shipped a fabricated success
    payload.** `ToolHandlerFor`'s generated handler marshals the typed
    `Out` value into `CallToolResult.StructuredContent` unconditionally
    whenever it is non-nil — the SDK never checks `IsError` first. Since
    every tool here returned a zero-valued `Out` struct (`GameOutput{}`,
    `WhoamiOutput{}`) alongside its hand-built error result, a
    `scope_violation` from `games.get` went out with
    `structuredContent: {"id":"","slug":"","name":""}` — a phantom game
    with an empty id — and `whoami` emitted a plausible-looking anonymous
    identity on every failure. The MCP spec tells a client that supports
    structured content to prefer it over the unstructured `Content`
    block, so a conforming agent would read the phantom and never see the
    error at all. The original `TestMCPEndToEndOverHTTP` missed this
    because it only ever decoded `Content[0]`, never `StructuredContent`.
    Fixed by registering every tool with the SDK's `Out` type parameter
    fixed to `any` (see `addScopedTool`, item 12 below) and returning a
    literal `nil`, not a zero struct, on every error path: `ToolHandlerFor`
    only marshals a non-nil `any`, so `nil` suppresses `StructuredContent`
    (and the JSON-in-`Content` fallback derived from it) entirely.
    `TestMCPEndToEndOverHTTP` now asserts `StructuredContent == nil` on
    the error path and decodes `StructuredContent` (not `Content[0]`) on
    the success path, so this exact regression fails loudly if it
    recurs.
11. **Wire-shadow types (`whoamiWire`, `gameWire`, `gamesGetInput` with a
    `string` id field, all from Correction 5) were removed.** The
    reviewer confirmed directly against the SDK that `AddTool` validates
    a tool's output against its *marshalled JSON*, not the Go value —
    Correction 5's diagnosis was right, but the fix it reached for was
    the wrong one: the inference limitation only means the SDK cannot
    *derive* a correct schema from a `uuid.UUID` field by reflection, not
    that a `uuid.UUID` field cannot validate. A **hand-written**
    `*jsonschema.Schema`, passed as `mcp.Tool.OutputSchema`, is validated
    against the same marshalled JSON — a `uuid.UUID` field, declared
    `"string"` by hand, round-trips correctly, because `uuid.UUID`
    already marshals to a JSON string regardless of what the SDK's
    reflection would have inferred. `WhoamiOutput` and `GameOutput` keep
    their original `uuid.UUID` fields; `mcp.go`'s hand-written
    `gameOutputSchema`/`gamesListOutputSchema`/`whoamiOutputSchema`
    describe them. This also delivers Correction 10 for free, since
    `Out=any` needs an explicit schema to validate or advertise anything
    regardless. Removing the wire-shadow types also closes a silent
    divergence risk the review named directly: nothing failed to compile
    when a domain type's field changed and its wire twin did not,
    because nothing checked the two stayed in sync — a hand-written
    schema is the only "wire shape" description left, so there is
    nothing left to drift out of sync with it silently.
12. **Added `addScopedTool[In, Out any]`, a generic tool-registration
    helper (`mcp.go`), because each of the three tools was re-deriving
    the same caller-resolve → scope-check → error-map → suppress-on-error
    sequence by hand.** Forgetting the caller check entirely fails
    loudly (a nil-pointer panic reading `Caller.UserID`), so that was
    never the real risk the review named; the real risk is a future
    metamodel tool that takes an entity or relation-type id and queries
    by it *without* a scope check at all — nothing in the original shape
    prevented that, and the original `games.get`, which took a bare
    `project_id` and used it as a lookup key, actively taught the wrong
    habit. `addScopedTool`'s handler signature receives the caller's own
    **resolved** project id (`uuid.UUID`), never a `Caller` — a metamodel
    tool built on it cannot reach for an unchecked id argument to query
    by, because the only project id it is ever handed is already the
    caller's own. A handler that needs more than the resolved project id
    (`whoami` needs the caller's own user id and admin flag) still has
    `ctx` and may call `CallerFrom(ctx)` itself — `addScopedTool` has
    already required a valid token caller to reach that point, so this
    cannot be used to route around the scope check, only to read
    identity the scope check has already vouched for.
13. **`project_id` became an optional, checked confirmation field
    instead of a lookup key, on all three tools uniformly (`ScopedArgs`,
    embedded in each tool's own input type).** The coordinator settled
    this as a deliberate middle position between two extremes: dropping
    the field entirely makes a wrong-project mistake structurally
    impossible, but also removes the one way an agent juggling two
    tokens for two games can state its intent and be told when it
    guessed wrong — Nottario requires an equivalent field for exactly
    that reason. Supplied, `addScopedTool` requires it to equal the
    token's own binding or fails with `scope_violation` before any
    handler runs; omitted, the tool acts on the token's own binding
    exactly as if it had not been asked. `games.get` no longer accepts
    an arbitrary project id to look up — it has no argument that selects
    which project to query at all, only the optional confirmation — so
    the shape that taught the wrong habit (Correction 12) no longer
    exists to copy.
14. **The error envelope grew a `details` object, and errors became
    self-describing.** A `map[string]string` (the original
    `mcpErrorResult`) cannot carry what the metamodel plan's own error
    vocabulary needs: a field path on `schema_violation`, the current
    version on a version conflict, both endpoint types on
    `endpoint_type_mismatch`. Added `MCPError` (`mcp_errors.go`, a typed
    error with `Code`, `Message` and an optional `Details map[string]any`)
    and had `mcpErrorFor` unwrap it with `errors.As` before falling back
    to its short list of known sentinels (`projects.ErrProjectNotFound`)
    and then to `errCodeInternal`. `ErrScopeViolation` is now an
    `*MCPError` built with `NewMCPError`, not a bare `errors.New`. A
    metamodel task adding a new failure mode defines its own `*MCPError`
    (optionally via `.WithDetails(...)`) rather than adding a case to
    `mcpErrorFor`'s switch.
    **The wire shape for a coded error, settled here for every later
    task to read rather than reinvent:**
    ```json
    {"error": "schema_violation", "message": "...", "details": {"field": "..."}}
    ```
    `details` is omitted entirely when a domain error carries none (as
    every error in this task's own three tools still does — none of them
    needs one yet).
    **The batch envelope for a future bulk (`atomic`/`partial`) write is
    decided now, for the same reason:** an items array, one entry per
    input item, each entry carrying its own index, an `ok` flag, and
    (when `ok` is false) an error in the same `{error, message, details}`
    shape as a single-call failure:
    ```json
    {"items": [{"index": 0, "ok": true}, {"index": 1, "ok": false, "error": "schema_violation", "message": "...", "details": {...}}]}
    ```
    No Go type for this exists yet — Task 13 has no bulk operation to
    return it from, and an unused exported type is exactly the dead-code
    shape this same review flagged elsewhere (item 16 below) — so this
    is written down as a contract for the metamodel task that adds the
    first bulk tool to implement, not as scaffolding to copy blind.
15. **Documented, and pinned with a test, the one place this surface's
    error vocabulary does not reach.** A missing or wrongly-typed
    argument is rejected by the SDK's own input-schema validation before
    any tool handler in this package ever runs, and comes back as plain
    prose (`err.Error()`) with no `"error"` code at all — `mcpErrorResult`
    cannot intercept a failure that happens before it is ever called.
    `mcpErrorResult`'s own doc comment now names this explicitly, and
    `TestMCPInputValidationFailuresAreProseNotACode`
    (`mcp_http_test.go`) sends a malformed argument through the real
    transport and asserts the response is *not* valid JSON in this
    package's error shape — pinning the exception instead of leaving a
    comment's claim unchecked.
16. **`games.list`'s output grew a pagination envelope
    (`GamesListOutput{Items, NextCursor, Truncated}`) it does not yet
    need, deliberately, to establish the shape before a tool that does
    need it exists.** There was no limit, cursor, or truncation flag
    anywhere on this surface, and `games.list` can only structurally
    ever return zero or one item (a token sees exactly its own bound
    game) — so this task never met the question `entities.list` and
    `search` will meet on day one. The envelope is the deliverable here;
    `games.list` itself has nothing to page through, so `Truncated` is
    always `false` and `NextCursor` always `nil` for this tool
    specifically, which `TestMCPGamesListReturnsExactlyTheTokensOneGame`
    now asserts. **For a metamodel list tool:** push the limit into the
    domain query itself (a `LIMIT`-bearing SQL call, not `deps.X.List(ctx)`
    followed by a Go-side slice) so the bound is structural, not a
    convention a handler could forget to apply after fetching everything.
17. **Minor fixes bundled into the same review pass:** the dead
    `GamesListOutput` domain type (declared, populated nowhere — the
    original wire wrapper it should have fed was built separately) is
    now the actual return type of `MCPGamesList` (see Correction 16);
    `mcpErrorFor`'s log line gained `tool`, `user_id` and `token_id`
    fields, since "an agent got `internal_error`" was not something an
    operator could previously search a log for; `mcpHandler` now wraps
    the request body in `http.MaxBytesReader` itself
    (`maxMCPRequestBodyBytes`, 4MiB) ahead of handing off to the SDK's
    own transport, rather than leaving that bound to the SDK's internal
    default indefinitely — sized well above `maxAuthRequestBodyBytes`
    because a future bulk upsert needs real headroom; all three tools
    now carry `ToolAnnotations{ReadOnlyHint: true, IdempotentHint: true}`,
    set while there are three tools and it is free rather than
    retrofitted once a write tool exists and the distinction starts to
    matter; `TestMCPGamesGetReportsNotFoundForAMissingProject` was
    renamed to `TestMCPGamesGetReturnsTheCallersOwnGame` (it looked up an
    existing project and asserted its slug — the not-found branch was
    never actually exercised) and a new
    `TestMCPGamesGetReportsNotFoundForAMissingProject` takes its old
    name and its old claim seriously; and every test in this task's own
    files that used to discard a setup call's error with `_` now checks
    it, so a broken fixture fails at the line that broke it, not several
    lines later with a confusing message.

**For the nine metamodel tasks that build directly on this scaffolding:**
a new tool registers through `addScopedTool` (Correction 12) rather than
resolving a `Caller` and deriving scope itself — the wrapper hands the
handler an already-resolved, already-checked project id, and a handler
that reaches for an entity or relation by id has no unchecked id
argument available to query by, by construction rather than convention.
**This only covers the id `addScopedTool` itself knows about.**
`ScopedArgs`'s `project_id` is the one field the wrapper checks; it
cannot check a *differently named* id a tool carries in its own input
and then uses as a lookup key — an `entity_id` or `type_id` queried
directly would be the original defect relocated under a name the helper
does not know, and the metamodel tools take exactly those ids. The
positive rule, stronger than "do not do that": every metamodel query
filters by the resolved project id, and that filter lives in the sqlc
query itself, not in Go. The metamodel tables all carry `project_id`
precisely so this is expressible in SQL — a query that cannot be
executed without a project id cannot be misused by a handler that forgot
one. `addScopedTool` hands the tool a resolved project id; the query
layer is where it becomes enforcement. The metamodel plan's own Task 7
(`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-31-metamodel.md`) states this as a
requirement of its queries directly, for the person who reads that plan
without first reading this one. Any id-carrying output type keeps its
natural Go type (`uuid.UUID` included)
and gets a **hand-written** `*jsonschema.Schema` passed as
`mcp.Tool.OutputSchema` (Correction 11) — never a second, hand-maintained
wire-shadow struct, and never the SDK's own reflection-based inference
for a type whose JSON marshalling comes from a method the SDK's
reflection does not consult. A new tool that accepts a project id at all
embeds `ScopedArgs` (Correction 13) so `project_id` is optional-but-
checked the same way on every tool, not a bespoke lookup parameter. A
new failure mode is an `*MCPError`, built with `NewMCPError` and
`.WithDetails(...)` when it needs to carry a field path, a version, or a
pair of endpoint types (Correction 14) — never a new case added to
`mcpErrorFor`'s switch, and the bulk-write envelope in that same
correction is the shape the first `atomic`/`partial` write tool should
return, not a shape to invent fresh. A new list tool grows
`GamesListOutput`'s own `{items, next_cursor, truncated}` shape
(Correction 16) and pushes its limit into the domain query, not a Go
slice operation after a full fetch. And the transport stays mounted
`Stateless` (Correction 7): every tool call this task or any later one
adds is its own independently re-authenticated HTTP request, so no tool
handler ever needs to reason about a token or session going stale
mid-call the way a long-lived connection would force it to.

---

### Task 14: Live updates over SSE

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/realtime/hub.go`, `internal/web/events.go`
- Modify: `internal/web/server.go`, `internal/db/migrations/0002_events.sql`
- Test: `internal/realtime/hub_test.go`

**Inherited constraint from Task 10: authentication is evaluated once, at
connection time.** `authenticate` (`internal/web/auth.go`) resolves a
Caller when the request first arrives and never again for that request's
lifetime — its own doc comment says so explicitly, because this is where
it first mattered. An SSE stream accepted under a valid Caller keeps
running under that same Caller for as long as the connection stays open:
it does not notice a logout, a password change, a token revocation, or
being removed from the project it is streaming, and a session stream
never slides its expiry forward the way an ordinary request would,
because `resolveSessionCaller` only runs once, at the handshake. This is
not a defect to fix in this task by making `authenticate` smarter; it is
a property of a stateless per-request middleware meeting a stateful
long-lived connection, and this task has to design around it explicitly —
a bounded maximum connection lifetime that forces periodic
reconnection (and so periodic re-authentication) is the straightforward
option, and probably the right default one, but the decision belongs
here, made on purpose, not discovered later as a bug report.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing test**

`internal/realtime/hub_test.go`:

```go
package realtime_test

import (
	"testing"
	"time"

	"github.com/google/uuid"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/realtime"
)

func TestSubscriberReceivesItsProjectEvents(t *testing.T) {
	hub := realtime.NewHub()
	project := uuid.New()

	sub := hub.Subscribe(project)
	defer hub.Unsubscribe(sub)

	hub.Publish(realtime.Event{ProjectID: project, Kind: "project.updated", Payload: `{"slug":"azeroth"}`})

	select {
	case got := <-sub.C:
		if got.Kind != "project.updated" {
			t.Fatalf("Kind = %q", got.Kind)
		}
	case <-time.After(time.Second):
		t.Fatal("timed out waiting for the event")
	}
}

func TestSubscriberIgnoresOtherProjects(t *testing.T) {
	hub := realtime.NewHub()
	mine, theirs := uuid.New(), uuid.New()

	sub := hub.Subscribe(mine)
	defer hub.Unsubscribe(sub)

	hub.Publish(realtime.Event{ProjectID: theirs, Kind: "project.updated"})

	select {
	case got := <-sub.C:
		t.Fatalf("received an event for another project: %+v", got)
	case <-time.After(100 * time.Millisecond):
	}
}

func TestPublishDoesNotBlockOnASlowSubscriber(t *testing.T) {
	hub := realtime.NewHub()
	project := uuid.New()
	sub := hub.Subscribe(project)
	defer hub.Unsubscribe(sub)

	// Fill the buffer and keep going: a stuck browser must not stall writers.
	done := make(chan struct{})
	go func() {
		for i := 0; i < 1000; i++ {
			hub.Publish(realtime.Event{ProjectID: project, Kind: "noise"})
		}
		close(done)
	}()

	select {
	case <-done:
	case <-time.After(2 * time.Second):
		t.Fatal("Publish blocked on a slow subscriber")
	}
}
```

- [ ] **Step 3: Write the implementation**

`internal/realtime/hub.go`:

```go
// Package realtime fans state changes out to connected browsers.
package realtime

import (
	"sync"

	"github.com/google/uuid"
)

// Event is one state change, scoped to a project.
type Event struct {
	ProjectID uuid.UUID `json:"-"`
	Kind      string    `json:"kind"`
	Payload   string    `json:"payload,omitempty"`
}

// Subscription is one connected browser.
type Subscription struct {
	ProjectID uuid.UUID
	C         chan Event
}

// Hub routes events to the subscriptions of their project.
type Hub struct {
	mu   sync.RWMutex
	subs map[*Subscription]struct{}
}

// NewHub builds an empty hub.
func NewHub() *Hub {
	return &Hub{subs: make(map[*Subscription]struct{})}
}

// Subscribe registers a listener for one project's events.
func (h *Hub) Subscribe(projectID uuid.UUID) *Subscription {
	sub := &Subscription{ProjectID: projectID, C: make(chan Event, 64)}
	h.mu.Lock()
	h.subs[sub] = struct{}{}
	h.mu.Unlock()
	return sub
}

// Unsubscribe removes a listener and closes its channel.
func (h *Hub) Unsubscribe(sub *Subscription) {
	h.mu.Lock()
	if _, ok := h.subs[sub]; ok {
		delete(h.subs, sub)
		close(sub.C)
	}
	h.mu.Unlock()
}

// Publish delivers an event to every subscription of its project. A listener
// whose buffer is full simply misses the event: a stalled browser must never
// block a database write.
func (h *Hub) Publish(ev Event) {
	h.mu.RLock()
	defer h.mu.RUnlock()
	for sub := range h.subs {
		if sub.ProjectID != ev.ProjectID {
			continue
		}
		select {
		case sub.C <- ev:
		default:
		}
	}
}
```

- [ ] **Step 5: Add the SSE endpoint**

`internal/web/events.go`:

```go
package web

import (
	"fmt"
	"net/http"
)

// handleEvents streams a project's changes to one browser.
func (s *Server) handleEvents(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request, caller Caller) {
	projectID, ok := s.requireProjectAccess(w, r, caller)
	if !ok {
		return
	}
	flusher, ok := w.(http.Flusher)
	if !ok {
		writeError(w, http.StatusInternalServerError, "internal_error", "streaming is not supported")
		return
	}

	w.Header().Set("Content-Type", "text/event-stream")
	w.Header().Set("Cache-Control", "no-cache")
	w.Header().Set("Connection", "keep-alive")
	w.WriteHeader(http.StatusOK)
	flusher.Flush()

	sub := s.hub.Subscribe(projectID)
	defer s.hub.Unsubscribe(sub)

	for {
		select {
		case <-r.Context().Done():
			return
		case ev, open := <-sub.C:
			if !open {
				return
			}
			_, _ = fmt.Fprintf(w, "event: %s\ndata: %s\n\n", ev.Kind, ev.Payload)
			flusher.Flush()
		}
	}
}
```

Register in `NewServer`:

```go
	s.hub = realtime.NewHub()
	s.mux.Handle("GET /api/games/{game}/events", requireCaller(s.handleEvents))
```

and add the `hub *realtime.Hub` field to `Server`.

### Task 15: Minimal web UI

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/web/static/index.html`, `internal/web/static/login.html`, `internal/web/static/game.html`, `internal/web/static/styles.css`, `internal/web/static/app.js`, `internal/web/static.go`
- Modify: `internal/web/server.go`
- Test: `internal/web/static_test.go`

**Serve the static tree outside the authenticated chain, or short-circuit
`authenticate` on its prefix, before registering `GET /static/` below.**
`authenticate` (Task 10) wraps the entire mux and, on every request,
tries a session-cookie lookup if one is present — a real database round
trip. A page pulling twenty embedded assets is twenty identical,
wasted session lookups once this task lands `GET /static/` and the
`/login`, `/g/{game}` routes below, since a browser sends its cookie on
every same-origin request regardless of whether the handler ever reads
`CallerFrom`. Registering `/static/` (and any other path this task adds
that never calls `requireCaller`) on a handler `authenticate` recognises
and skips — a prefix check before the bearer/cookie branches in
`internal/web/auth.go` is the straightforward shape — closes that
before it ships with real assets behind it, rather than after someone
notices the extra load on `sessions`.

**This task also owns "last visited game" — a genuine spec requirement
with no owner anywhere in the plan until now.** The design document
says a user who has been going straight into one game for months
should land where they expect the moment a second game appears; Task
12's `handleRoot` only ever answers "zero, one, or more" and
deliberately does not implement this (see that task's own corrections,
item 9). It belongs here, client-side, not as a server column: a
column would cost a write on every navigation for a pure convenience,
and worse, a remembered value can go stale the moment the user loses
access to that game or it is deleted — a server column has no natural
place to notice either has happened. `localStorage` costs nothing per
navigation and is checked against a live answer every time it matters
(see the rule below), so staleness is never observable.

The rule: `game.html`'s script records the slug it was loaded for in
`localStorage` under `maestro:lastGame` every time it runs. `index.html`'s
picker script — reached only when `handleRoot` did *not* find exactly
one game, i.e. zero or several — fetches `GET /api/games` exactly as it
already does, and only *after* that response comes back does it read
the remembered slug and check whether it appears in the list the server
just returned. It redirects to `/g/{slug}` only on that match; a
remembered slug absent from the fresh list (the game was deleted, or
the user lost membership) is silently ignored and the ordinary picker
renders instead. Never redirect off the stored value alone, before or
without that corroboration — that is precisely what would let stale
browser memory point at a game the user can no longer reach.
`handleRoot` itself needs no change for this: it still only ever needs
to know zero, one, or more.

`internal/web/static/app.js`:

```js
// Minimal progressive behaviour. No build step, no framework yet: the real
// interface arrives with the views sub-project.

// Where the picker remembers which game this browser last visited, so a
// user who has been going straight into one game for months lands where
// they expect the moment a second game appears and the server-side
// single-game shortcut (handleRoot, internal/web/api_projects.go) stops
// applying. See this task's own note above for why this lives here,
// client-side, instead of a server column.
const LAST_GAME_KEY = "maestro:lastGame";

// localStorage can throw (private browsing, a full quota, a disabled
// storage API) — losing this convenience must never break the page it
// is a convenience *for*, so every access goes through these two
// wrappers rather than being called inline.
function rememberGame(slug) {
  try {
    localStorage.setItem(LAST_GAME_KEY, slug);
  } catch {
    // Nothing to do: the next visit just won't have a remembered slug.
  }
}
function recallGame() {
  try {
    return localStorage.getItem(LAST_GAME_KEY);
  } catch {
    return null;
  }
}

const loginForm = document.getElementById("login");
if (loginForm) {
  loginForm.addEventListener("submit", async (event) => {
    event.preventDefault();
    const data = new FormData(loginForm);
    const response = await fetch("/api/auth/login", {
      method: "POST",
      headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" },
      body: JSON.stringify({
        email: data.get("email"),
        password: data.get("password"),
      }),
    });
    if (response.ok) {
      window.location.href = "/";
      return;
    }
    document.getElementById("error").textContent = "Email or password is wrong.";
  });
}

// game.html has no #games list; index.html's picker does. Splitting on
// that, rather than the URL, keeps this one file shared by both pages
// without either needing to know which page loaded it.
const gamesList = document.getElementById("games");
if (gamesList) {
  const response = await fetch("/api/games");
  const body = await response.json();
  const games = body.games ?? [];

  // Redirect to the remembered game only once the server's own response
  // has just confirmed the user can still reach it — never off the
  // stored value alone. A slug that is no longer in this list (the game
  // was deleted, or membership was lost) is silently ignored and the
  // ordinary picker renders instead, rather than sending the user
  // toward a game that no longer answers for them.
  const remembered = recallGame();
  if (remembered && games.some((game) => game.slug === remembered)) {
    window.location.href = `/g/${remembered}`;
  } else {
    for (const game of games) {
      const item = document.createElement("li");
      const link = document.createElement("a");
      link.href = `/g/${game.slug}`;
      link.textContent = game.name;
      item.append(link);
      gamesList.append(item);
    }
  }
}

// game.html: record the slug this page was loaded for. There is no
// server-side slug resolution on this route (Task 8's Round 2
// Correction 12 — /g/{game} only ever serves this static shell), so the
// slug is read from the URL the browser already has rather than from
// any response body.
const gameSlugMatch = window.location.pathname.match(/^\/g\/([^/]+)/);
if (gameSlugMatch) {
  rememberGame(gameSlugMatch[1]);
}
```

- [ ] **Step 4: Serve them from the binary**

`internal/web/static.go`:

```go
package web

import (
	"embed"
	"io/fs"
	"net/http"
	"path"
	"strings"
)

//go:embed static
var staticFS embed.FS

// assets is the embedded static tree rooted at static/.
var assets = func() fs.FS {
	sub, err := fs.Sub(staticFS, "static")
	if err != nil {
		panic(err)
	}
	return sub
}()

// serveAsset writes one embedded file with the right content type.
func (s *Server) serveAsset(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request, name string) {
	body, err := fs.ReadFile(assets, name)
	if err != nil {
		http.NotFound(w, r)
		return
	}
	switch strings.ToLower(path.Ext(name)) {
	case ".html":
		w.Header().Set("Content-Type", "text/html; charset=utf-8")
	case ".css":
		w.Header().Set("Content-Type", "text/css; charset=utf-8")
	case ".js":
		w.Header().Set("Content-Type", "text/javascript; charset=utf-8")
	}
	_, _ = w.Write(body)
}
```

Delete the stub `serveAsset` added in Task 12, and register in `NewServer`:

```go
	s.mux.HandleFunc("GET /login", func(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) { s.serveAsset(w, r, "login.html") })
	s.mux.HandleFunc("GET /g/{game}", func(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) { s.serveAsset(w, r, "game.html") })
	s.mux.Handle("GET /static/", http.StripPrefix("/static/", http.FileServerFS(assets)))
```

**This task owns the expired-row sweep.** `identity.Service.PruneExpiredSessions`
(Task 6) and `identity.Service.PruneExpiredInvites` (Task 7) both exist,
both are tested, and both are deliberately left uncalled by their own
tasks with a comment pointing at "a later task" — process-lifecycle wiring
(a ticker, or an operator-run maintenance command) belongs with the rest of

later task: `run()` below must start a periodic sweep that calls both
before Task 16 is considered done, not leave the comment pointing
somewhere else again.

- [ ] **Step 1: Wire the process**

`cmd/maestro/main.go`:

```go
// Command maestro runs the Maestro server.
package main

import (
	"context"
	"errors"
	"log/slog"
	"net/http"
	"os"
	"os/signal"
	"syscall"
	"time"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/config"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/db"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/identity"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/projects"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/version"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/web"
)

func main() {
	if err := run(); err != nil {
		slog.Error("maestro stopped", "error", err)
		os.Exit(1)
	}
}

func run() error {
	cfg, err := config.Load(os.Getenv)
	if err != nil {
		return err
	}

	ctx, stop := signal.NotifyContext(context.Background(), syscall.SIGINT, syscall.SIGTERM)
	defer stop()

	pool, err := db.NewPool(ctx, cfg.DatabaseURL)
	if err != nil {
		return err
	}
	defer pool.Close()

	if err := db.Migrate(ctx, pool); err != nil {
		return err
	}

	ids := identity.New(pool, cfg)
	if err := ids.BootstrapFirstAdmin(ctx); err != nil {
		return err
	}

	srv := &http.Server{
		Addr: cfg.Addr,
		Handler: web.NewServer(web.Options{
			Version:  version.Version,
			Config:   cfg,
			Identity: ids,
			Projects: projects.New(pool),
		}),
		ReadHeaderTimeout: 10 * time.Second,
	}

	go func() {
		<-ctx.Done()
		shutdownCtx, cancel := context.WithTimeout(context.Background(), 15*time.Second)
		defer cancel()
		_ = srv.Shutdown(shutdownCtx)
	}()

	slog.Info("maestro listening", "addr", cfg.Addr, "version", version.Version)
	if err := srv.ListenAndServe(); err != nil && !errors.Is(err, http.ErrServerClosed) {
		return err
	}
	return nil
}
```

FROM golang:1.25-alpine AS build
WORKDIR /src
COPY go.mod go.sum ./
RUN go mod download
COPY . .
ARG VERSION=dev
RUN CGO_ENABLED=0 go build \
      -ldflags "-s -w -X github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/version.Version=${VERSION}" \
      -o /out/maestro ./cmd/maestro

FROM gcr.io/distroless/static-debian12:nonroot
COPY --from=build /out/maestro /maestro
EXPOSE 8080
USER nonroot:nonroot
ENTRYPOINT ["/maestro"]
```

services:
  maestro:
    build:
      context: .
      args:
        VERSION: dev
    environment:
      DATABASE_URL: postgres://maestro:maestro@db:5432/maestro?sslmode=disable
      # config.Load rejects this literal placeholder outright; generate a
      # real value with `openssl rand -base64 32` before deploying.
      SESSION_KEY: change-me-change-me-change-me-32ch
      FIRST_ADMIN_EMAIL: admin@example.com
      FIRST_ADMIN_PASSWORD: change-me-please
      REGISTRATION_MODE: invite_only

  db:

    volumes:

    healthcheck:

volumes:

### Task 17: Game deletion

**Files:**
- Modify: `internal/db/queries/projects.sql`, `internal/projects/projects.go`, `internal/web/api_projects.go`, `internal/web/server.go`
- Test: `internal/projects/projects_test.go`, `internal/web/api_projects_test.go`

**Why this task exists.** Task 8's last-owner guard (its own Go-side
check plus migration 0002's database trigger) means a project's sole
owner can neither demote nor remove themselves — `SetRole` and
`RemoveMember` both refuse to let a project drop to zero owners. Task
12 built the handlers for both, and its own corrections named the
resulting dead end explicitly: with the guard in place and no
`DeleteProject` anywhere in the plan, a designer who creates a game
alone has no way to ever leave it or delete it — the only exit is an
operator in `psql`. The guard and the exit belong together; this task
is the exit.

**The cascade: checked, not assumed.** `internal/db/migrations/0001_identity.sql`
already declares every foreign key from a table this plan defines
toward `projects` as `ON DELETE CASCADE`: `memberships.project_id`,
`api_tokens.project_id`, and `invites.project_id` (nullable, for an
unbound invite, but cascading wherever set). Migration 0002's
last-owner trigger was built with this exact statement in mind — its
own comment says so — and its escape hatch
(`IF NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM projects WHERE id = OLD.project_id) THEN RETURN NULL`)
exists specifically so a project's own deletion can cascade through its
last owner's membership row without tripping the very guard that
motivated this task. That means a plain `DELETE FROM projects WHERE id
= $1` already removes every membership, token and bound invite scoped
to the game in one statement, with no hand-rolled transaction needed on
the Go side to clean up after it.

This plan's own stated goal (see its opening line) is "no game-design
domain yet" — entity types, entities and relations do not exist
anywhere in this codebase or this plan, so there is nothing to check
for them today. A future plan that adds them must give their own
`project_id` foreign keys the same `ON DELETE CASCADE` convention this
task relies on; if one of them does not, `Service.Delete` below stops
being sufficient on its own and must become a transaction that deletes
that table's rows explicitly before deleting the project — written down
here so that future task does not have to rediscover this task's own
reasoning from scratch.

func TestDeleteProjectCascadesMembershipsAndTokens(t *testing.T) {
	svc, ids := newTestServices(t)
	ctx := context.Background()

	owner, _ := ids.CreateUser(ctx, identity.CreateUserRequest{Email: "owner@studio.com", DisplayName: "Owner", Password: "password12345"})
	project, err := svc.Create(ctx, "azeroth", "Azeroth", owner.ID)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("Create: %v", err)
	}
	if _, _, err := ids.CreateAPIToken(ctx, identity.CreateAPITokenRequest{ProjectID: project.ID, UserID: owner.ID, Label: "agent"}); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("CreateAPIToken: %v", err)
	}

	if err := svc.Delete(ctx, project.ID); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("Delete: %v", err)
	}

	if _, err := svc.ByID(ctx, project.ID); !errors.Is(err, projects.ErrProjectNotFound) {
		t.Fatalf("ByID after delete: err = %v, want ErrProjectNotFound", err)
	}
	if _, err := svc.RoleOf(ctx, owner.ID, project.ID); !errors.Is(err, projects.ErrNotAMember) {
		t.Fatalf("RoleOf after delete: err = %v, want ErrNotAMember (membership must be gone)", err)
	}
}
```

func TestOwnerCanDeleteGame(t *testing.T) {
	srv, ids, projSvc := newTestServer(t)
	ctx := context.Background()

	owner, _ := ids.CreateUser(ctx, identity.CreateUserRequest{Email: "owner@studio.com", DisplayName: "Owner", Password: "password12345"})
	project, _ := projSvc.Create(ctx, "azeroth", "Azeroth", owner.ID)

	req.AddCookie(cookie)
	rec := httptest.NewRecorder()

	if rec.Code != http.StatusNoContent {

	}
}

		t.Fatalf("status = %d, want 403", rec.Code)
	}
}

func TestDeletingGameRevokesItsTokens(t *testing.T) {
	srv, ids, projSvc := newTestServer(t)
	ctx := context.Background()

	owner, _ := ids.CreateUser(ctx, identity.CreateUserRequest{Email: "owner@studio.com", DisplayName: "Owner", Password: "password12345"})
	project, _ := projSvc.Create(ctx, "azeroth", "Azeroth", owner.ID)
	token, _, err := ids.CreateAPIToken(ctx, identity.CreateAPITokenRequest{ProjectID: project.ID, UserID: owner.ID, Label: "agent"})
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("CreateAPIToken: %v", err)
	}
	cookie := loginAs(t, srv, "owner@studio.com")

	req := httptest.NewRequest(http.MethodDelete, "/api/games/"+project.ID.String(), nil)
	req.AddCookie(cookie)
	rec := httptest.NewRecorder()
	srv.ServeHTTP(rec, req)
	if rec.Code != http.StatusNoContent {
		t.Fatalf("delete status = %d, want 204: %s", rec.Code, rec.Body.String())
	}

	meReq := httptest.NewRequest(http.MethodGet, "/api/me", nil)
	meReq.Header.Set("Authorization", "Bearer "+token)
	meRec := httptest.NewRecorder()
	srv.ServeHTTP(meRec, meReq)
	if meRec.Code != http.StatusUnauthorized {
		t.Fatalf("token after game deletion: status = %d, want 401", meRec.Code)
	}
}

- [ ] **Step 3: Add the query and the service method**

Append to `internal/db/queries/projects.sql`:

```sql
-- name: DeleteProject :exec
DELETE FROM projects WHERE id = $1;
```

Append to `internal/projects/projects.go`:

```go
// Delete removes a project outright. Every membership, API token and
// bound invite scoped to it disappears in the same statement, through
// the ON DELETE CASCADE foreign keys migration 0001 already declares on
// each — see this task's own plan section for why that is enough on its
// own, with no hand-rolled transaction here. Migration 0002's last-owner
// trigger has an explicit escape hatch for exactly this statement (see
// its own comment): deleting a project cascades to its memberships,
// including its last owner's, and the trigger does not fire for a
// membership whose project is already gone — so this never trips the
// very guard the rest of this package enforces everywhere else.
//
// Unlike RemoveMember and RevokeSession, deleting an id that does not
// exist is not folded into a silent no-op here: the caller (Task 17's
// HTTP handler) has already resolved projectID from a route it holds
// standing on, so a project that turns out not to exist is worth
// surfacing as a genuine anomaly rather than swallowing quietly.
func (s *Service) Delete(ctx context.Context, projectID uuid.UUID) error {
	if err := s.q.DeleteProject(ctx, projectID); err != nil {
		return fmt.Errorf("delete project: %w", err)
	}
	return nil
}
```
